Module 1 Source code

<li>Setting up a searchable image library on the translator's computer (Paratext method)</li>
<li>Finding and downloading free CC-licensed images from openbibleimages.org</li>
<li>Searching for relevant images by keyword and Bible reference</li>
<li>Inserting images with proper captions and copyright information</li>
<li>Making decisions about image placement and sizing</li>
</ul>
</div>

<div class="card-header">
<h3>Prerequisites</h3>
</div>
<div class="card-content" contenteditable="true">
<p>Before starting this module, ensure:</p>
<ul>
<li>The translation is finalised and approved</li>
<li>The translator has internet access</li>
<li>The translator has sufficient storage space in their Pictures folder</li>
<li>The translator has editing permissions for the Paratext project</li>
</ul>
</div>
</div>
<h2>Introduction</h2>
<p><em>Time: 5 minutes</em></p>
<p>The translation work is complete and the consultants have approved the text. Now the typesetter is asking: "What about illustrations?" Visual elements aren't just decoration — they help readers understand unfamiliar cultural contexts, aid comprehension for less-literate readers, and make the Scripture more engaging.</p>
<p>This module guides you through the complete process of adding illustrations to a Paratext project. It covers two image sources: the SIL Image and Map Repository (downloaded once to the computer) and Open Bible Images, which offers freely licensed images searchable and downloadable directly from the web.</p>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #e6a817; border-left: 6px solid #e6a817; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #fffdf0; align-items: flex-start; color: #3d3000;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>WARNING</strong>
<p><strong>Important principle: Only insert small jpg files (or file names) into the Paratext project.</strong> When the project goes to the typesetter for final composition, these small files will be replaced with high-resolution versions. This keeps the project file size manageable during the translation process.</p>
</div>
</div>
<hr style="border: 2px solid black;">
<h2>Procedure Walkthrough</h2>
<p><em>Time: 35 minutes</em></p>
<h3>Understanding the SIL Image and Map Library</h3>
<p>The SIL library provides access to over 2,800 illustrations covering biblical events, cultural contexts, geography, and objects. These images are available in both colour and black-and-white versions, and they're indexed for easy searching.</p>
<h3>Choosing Your Image Source</h3>
<p>There are two approaches for finding images to insert into the Paratext project. You can use either one, or a combination of both:</p>
<table style="width: 100%; border-collapse: collapse; margin: 1em 0;">
<thead>
<tr style="background-color: #f3f5f9;">
<th style="padding: 10px 8px; text-align: left; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Feature</th>
<th style="padding: 10px 8px; text-align: left; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Option A: SIL Image and Map Repository<br><small>(<a href="https://software.sil.org/illustrations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">software.sil.org/illustrations/</a>)</small></th>
<th style="padding: 10px 8px; text-align: left; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Option B: Open Bible Images<br><small>(openbibleimages.org)</small></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;"><strong>Connectivity</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Offline (after initial download)</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Online only</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;"><strong>Style</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Traditional Line Art / BW</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Varied (Photos, Paintings, Art)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;"><strong>Licensing</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Pre-cleared for Paratext</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">CC (requires specific attribution)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;"><strong>High-Res</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Handled by Typesetter</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Downloaded by you</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #3a9bd5; border-left: 6px solid #3a9bd5; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f0f8ff; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a2d3d;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>INFO</strong>
<p><strong>Which option should I choose?</strong> For most projects following the standard Paratext workflow, Option A remains the default — it is well-integrated with the typesetting pipeline. Option B is a good choice when you want images with no licensing restrictions, when the community prefers a different visual style, or when the SIL library does not have a suitable image for a specific passage. Many teams use both: Option A for the majority of images and Option B to fill gaps.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Before the session, ask the translation team whether they have reliable internet access. If connectivity is poor or intermittent, plan to use Option A as the primary method and demonstrate Option B as a secondary tool for when the internet is available.</p>
</div>
</div>

<div class="tab-header" data-widget-part="tab-header" data-widget-action="tab-switch" data-tab-index="2" contenteditable="true">Part 1C: Decide on Image Placement</div>

<div class="tab-content active" data-widget-part="tab-content" data-tab-index="0" style="display: block !important;" contenteditable="true">
<h4>Part 1A: Setting Up and Searching — SIL Image Repository (Windows)</h4>
<p>This part takes place in Windows, before opening Paratext. The translator needs to download the image library and learn to search it in File Explorer.</p>
<h5>Step 1: Download and Set Up the Image Library</h5>
<p><strong>First time only:</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>Open <a href="https://tiny.cc/sampleimages" target="_blank" rel="noopener">https://tiny.cc/sampleimages</a> in the web browser</li>
<li>Right-click on the folder <strong>Illustrations – English</strong> and select Download (approximately 121 MB — may take several minutes)</li>
<li>Once downloaded, locate the zip file in the Downloads folder</li>
<li>Open/extract the zip file and find the single folder inside: <strong>Illustrations – English</strong></li>
<li>Move this entire folder to the Pictures folder (<code>C:\Users\[YourName]\Pictures</code>)</li>
<li>Windows will automatically start indexing the image contents for searching</li>
</ol>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>The indexing process takes a few minutes after moving the folder. If the translator tries to search immediately and gets no results, ask them to wait 10–15 minutes and try again. This is a common source of confusion — it's not a mistake they made, just a timing issue. This setup only needs to be done once. If training multiple team members, one person can set this up and share the folder via network or USB drive.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h5>Step 2: Learn to Search for Images</h5>
<p><strong>Open the image folder:</strong> navigate to Pictures → Illustrations – English in File Explorer. All images will be displayed.</p>
<p><strong>Search by keyword:</strong> click in the search field (top right of File Explorer) and type descriptive words: cross, sheep, house, boat, temple. The search shows all images whose metadata includes that keyword.</p>
<p><strong>Search by Bible reference:</strong> use the book abbreviation + two-digit chapter number (e.g., MAT27, ACT03, JHN11, GEN01). This shows images tagged for that chapter.</p>
<p><strong>Search by visual style:</strong> add <strong>black</strong> to your search to see only black-and-white images, or <strong>color</strong> to see only colour images. Example: <strong>cross black</strong> shows black-and-white images of crosses.</p>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>TIP</strong>
<p>Use multiple search terms together — try "MAT27 cross" to find cross images specifically for Matthew 27, or "temple color" to find colour images of the temple.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Image selection is primarily a cultural decision — let the translator and team lead this conversation. Your role is to help them search effectively and ensure they understand the practical constraints (32-page limit, colour vs black-and-white implications). Don't express preferences about which images look better — that judgment belongs to the translation team.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="tab-content" data-widget-part="tab-content" data-tab-index="1" style="display: none !important;" contenteditable="true">
<h4>Part 1B: Finding Images Online — Open Bible Images</h4>
<p>Open Bible Images (<a href="https://openbibleimages.org" target="_blank" rel="noopener">openbibleimages.org</a>) is a website offering Scripture illustrations under Creative Commons (CC) licensing. Images can be used freely in published Bibles and other Scripture materials without requesting separate permission — you simply follow the attribution terms of the specific CC license shown for each image.</p>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #3a9bd5; border-left: 6px solid #3a9bd5; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f0f8ff; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a2d3d;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>INFO</strong>
<p>Because Open Bible Images uses CC licensing, teams can download high-resolution images and send them directly to the typesetter without waiting for copyright clearance. This can simplify and speed up the publication process, particularly for smaller or under-resourced projects.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h5>Step 1B-1: Browse and Search the Website</h5>
<ol>
<li>Open a web browser and navigate to <a href="https://openbibleimages.org" target="_blank" rel="noopener">https://openbibleimages.org</a></li>
<li>Use the search bar to find images by Scripture passage (e.g., "John", "Mark 1"), topic or keyword (e.g., "pastor", "prayer", "cross"), or biblical event (e.g., "baptism of Jesus", "The Last Supper")</li>
<li>Browse the results and click on any image to see the full-size preview and its license details</li>
</ol>
<h5>Step 1B-2: Check the License</h5>
<p>Before using an image, note the specific Creative Commons license displayed on the image page:</p>
<table style="width: 100%; border-collapse: collapse; margin: 1em 0;">
<thead>
<tr style="background-color: #f3f5f9;">
<th style="padding: 10px 8px; text-align: left; border: 1px solid #ccc;">License</th>
<th style="padding: 10px 8px; text-align: left; border: 1px solid #ccc;">What it means for your project</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">CC BY</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Free to use in any format. Credit the creator in your copyright notice.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">CC BY-SA</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Free to use. Credit the creator, and distribute the final publication under a similar open licence.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">CC BY-NC</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Free for non-commercial use. Suitable for most Bible translation publications; confirm with your Translation Consultant if unsure.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">CC0</td>
<td style="padding: 6px 8px; border: 1px solid #ccc;">Public domain dedication. No attribution required, though it's courteous to credit the creator.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #e6a817; border-left: 6px solid #e6a817; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #fffdf0; align-items: flex-start; color: #3d3000;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>WARNING — CC BY-NC</strong>
<p>Most Bible translation projects are non-commercial ministry work and will qualify for CC BY-NC use. However, if <em>any</em> money changes hands for the physical book, the CC BY-NC licence might be risky. It's safer to stick to CC BY, CC BY-SA, or CC0. If unsure, check with your Translation Consultant before using images under this licence. Remind the team that 'Non-Commercial' is often interpreted strictly by secular copyright holders. If the local church plans to sell these Bibles to cover costs, strongly recommend sticking to CC0 or CC BY images to avoid future legal friction.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h5>Step 1B-3: Download the Image</h5>
<ol>
<li>On the image page, look for the download option (button or right-click to save)</li>
<li>Save the image to a dedicated folder: <strong>Pictures → Illustrations – Open Bible Images</strong></li>
<li>Use a consistent naming convention, e.g., <code>JHN03_Nicodemus.jpg</code></li>
<li>Note the full attribution text shown on the image page — you will need this for the Copyright field in Paratext</li>
</ol>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>TIP</strong>
<p>Copy the attribution from the Open Bible Images website and paste it into a notepad document alongside the filename before you begin inserting images in Paratext. This saves time later when filling in the Copyright field.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Open Bible Images is an online-only resource. If the translator's internet connection is slow or unreliable, it is better to download images in advance when connectivity is good, rather than browsing the website during the training session.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="tab-content" data-widget-part="tab-content" data-tab-index="2" style="display: none !important;" contenteditable="true">
<h4>Part 1C: Decide on Image Placement</h4>
<p>This step applies regardless of which image source you use. Before inserting images, the team needs to consider the following.</p>
<h5>How many illustrations?</h5>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #e6a817; border-left: 6px solid #e6a817; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #fffdf0; align-items: flex-start; color: #3d3000;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>WARNING — 32-Page Limit</strong>
<p>You can only have 32 pages of images in a New Testament. This is a physical printing constraint and is also set by the funders. Help the team plan their illustrations carefully — prioritise the most important passages and culturally significant images.</p>
</div>
</div>
<ul>
<li>Too many images can distract from the text; too few may miss opportunities to aid comprehension</li>
<li>Consider the readers' literacy level and learning preferences</li>
<li>Discuss with the translation team and community advisors</li>
<li>Consult with the Translation Consultant if guidance is needed on the appropriate quantity</li>
</ul>
<h5>Where to place images?</h5>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>TIP — Where to place your cursor</strong>
<p>Always place your cursor at the end of the verse where the image should appear. This 'anchors' the image to that specific verse even if the text flows to a new page.</p>
</div>
</div>
<p>Good placement choices: key events (crucifixion, resurrection, parables), culturally unfamiliar objects (temple layout, ancient tools, clothing), geographic contexts (maps, landscape features), and teaching moments (visual aids for difficult concepts).</p>
<h5>What size and colour?</h5>
<ul>
<li><strong>Column width</strong> — image fits within one column of text (most common)</li>
<li><strong>Page width</strong> — image spans across columns or full page (use sparingly for major events)</li>
<li><strong>Black-and-white images</strong> — printed on the page with the text (inline)</li>
<li><strong>Colour images</strong> — printed as full-page images inserted together in a signature (separate section)</li>
</ul>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #999999; border-left: 6px solid #999999; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f9f9f9; align-items: flex-start; color: #333333;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>NOTE</strong>
<p>The choice between colour and black-and-white affects both cost and layout. Colour images are grouped separately, while black-and-white images appear inline with text. Discuss with the typesetter early to understand the implications for budget and design.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>The 32-page limit and the colour vs black-and-white decision are the two things the team most often don't know about in advance. Raise both of these early — before the team gets attached to a long list of images — to avoid disappointment later. If the team is unsure how many images to use, suggest they draft a provisional list and review it with the Translation Consultant before inserting anything.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<h2>Part 2: Inserting Images in Paratext</h2>
<p>Once the team has identified which images to use and where to place them, move into Paratext to insert them. This process is the same whether you are using images from Option A or Option B.</p>
<h3>Step 4: Insert an Illustration in Paratext</h3>
<ol>
<li>Open the Paratext project</li>
<li>Navigate to the verse where the image should appear and place your cursor at the end of the verse</li>
<li>From the <strong>Insert</strong> menu, choose <strong>Figure</strong> — the Figure Properties dialog box will open</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Understanding the Figure Properties dialog — enter the following:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>[1] Caption:</strong> enter the text that should appear below the image in the <strong>translation language</strong></li>
<li><strong>[2] Description:</strong> enter a description in the <strong>typesetter's language</strong> (e.g., English, French, Spanish) — helps the typesetter verify the correct image is being used</li>
<li><strong>[3] Width:</strong> usually <strong>Single column</strong>; use <strong>Full page width</strong> only for very important or detailed images</li>
<li><strong>[4] Location:</strong> specify where the image should appear (P = Print, E = Electronic, W = Web). If you want the image to show up everywhere, leave this field <strong>blank</strong></li>
<li><strong>[5] Copyright:</strong> SIL Library — copy the info from the filename. Open Bible Images — paste the attribution text you saved earlier</li>
<li><strong>[6] Reference:</strong> filled automatically by Paratext based on where your cursor was (e.g., MAT 27:1). <strong>DO NOT EDIT</strong></li>
<li><strong>[7] Browse:</strong> click to find and select your image file from your computer</li>
</ul>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>The Figure dialog can look overwhelming at first. Guide the translator through it one field at a time. The caption field is where the translator needs to think carefully — give them time to craft this in their language rather than rushing to the next step. The description should be in the typesetter's language. The copyright field is easy to miss — make sure it gets filled in correctly every time. Before the team inserts many images, have them insert 2–3 samples and get feedback from the Translation Consultant on caption style. It's much easier to adjust the approach early than to revise dozens of captions later.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h3>Step 5: Browse for the Image File</h3>
<ol>
<li>Click the <strong>Browse…</strong> button [7]</li>
<li>In the left sidebar, navigate to <strong>Pictures</strong></li>
<li>Open the appropriate folder: Option A — <strong>Illustrations – English</strong>; Option B — <strong>Illustrations – Open Bible Images</strong></li>
<li>Click in the Search field (top right) and type keywords to filter the images</li>
<li>Click on the desired image to select it, then click <strong>Open</strong></li>
<li>You'll return to the Figure dialog with the filename now filled in</li>
<li>Review all entries, then click <strong>OK</strong> to insert the image</li>
<li><em>The image (or a string of USFM codes starting with \fig) will now appear in the text at the specified location</em></li>
<li>If you can't see the image, change the view to <strong>Formatted</strong> or <strong>Preview</strong></li>
</ol>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Ensure the translator doesn't rename the images after inserting them in Paratext or the link will break. If they must move their project to a different computer, the <code>Illustrations</code> folder must be in the exact same file path (e.g., <code>C:\Users\[Name]\Pictures...</code>) or the links will break there too.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h3>Tips for Efficient Image Insertion</h3>
<p><strong>Make searching easier:</strong> double-click the title bar to maximise the file selection window; switch to "Very Large Icons" view to see images clearly; hide the preview pane to see more images at once.</p>
<p><strong>Maintain consistency:</strong> use similar caption styles throughout the project; if mixing images from both sources, ensure the visual styles are compatible; be consistent with black-and-white vs. colour choices.</p>
<p><strong>Work efficiently:</strong> plan all illustrations first (make a list, noting source for each); download any Open Bible Images in advance; insert multiple images in one work session; review all images together to ensure consistency.</p>
<hr style="border: 2px solid black;"><!-- Card: Verification Checklist -->
<div class="ui-card ui-component" data-widget-type="card" data-type="info" data-widget-id="widget-35-1786465062039">
<div class="drag-handle" title="Drag to reorder" role="button" tabindex="0"><i class="fa-solid fa-grip-dots-vertical"></i></div>
<div class="component-topbar">
<div class="component-type-badge" data-widget-action="menu-toggle"><span class="widget-move-buttons"> <button type="button" class="widget-move-btn move-up" data-widget-action="move-up" title="Move up"> <i class="fas fa-chevron-up"></i> </button> <button type="button" class="widget-move-btn move-down" data-widget-action="move-down" title="Move down"> <i class="fas fa-chevron-down"></i> </button> </span> Card <button class="component-menu-trigger" title="Widget Options"> <i class="fas fa-ellipsis-v"></i> </button></div>
</div>
<div class="card-header">
<h3>Verification Checklist</h3>
</div>
<div class="card-content" contenteditable="true">
<p><em>Time: 5 minutes — after completing this module, verify that the translator can:</em></p>
<p><strong>Option A — SIL Image Repository:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Successfully search the image library by keyword</li>
<li>Search for images by Bible reference (e.g., MAT27)</li>
<li>Filter search results by black-and-white or colour</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Option B — Open Bible Images:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Navigate to openbibleimages.org and use the search function</li>
<li>Identify and record the CC licence for a chosen image</li>
<li>Download an image and save it to the correct folder</li>
<li>Copy the attribution text for use in the Copyright field</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Inserting in Paratext (both options):</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Open the Figure dialog from the Insert menu</li>
<li>Enter a caption in the translation language</li>
<li>Enter a description in the typesetter's language</li>
<li>Choose appropriate image sizing (column vs. page width)</li>
<li>Browse and select an image file</li>
<li>Insert the correct copyright/attribution text</li>
<li>Insert the complete figure into the project and see it displayed at the correct verse</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Quality check:</strong> are captions clear and concise? Are descriptions helpful? Is copyright/attribution information included for every image? Are images placed at appropriate locations? Are images from different sources visually consistent?</p>
</div>
</div>
<hr style="border: 2px solid black;">
<h2>Troubleshooting</h2>
<p><em>Time: 5 minutes</em></p>
<p><strong>Problem: "Can't find the Illustrations – English folder"</strong><br>Solution: Check the Downloads folder for the zip file. It may need to be extracted again and moved to Pictures.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "Search isn't finding any images even though they exist"</strong><br>Solution: Windows may still be indexing the folder. Wait 10–15 minutes and try again, or check Indexing Options in Windows settings to ensure the Pictures folder is being indexed.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "Can't access openbibleimages.org"</strong><br>Solution: Check internet connectivity. If unavailable, switch to Option A for this session and plan to use Open Bible Images in the next session when connectivity is available.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "I'm not sure which CC licence is appropriate"</strong><br>Solution: When in doubt, consult your Translation Consultant. CC BY and CC BY-SA are clearly appropriate for most non-commercial Bible translation work. CC BY-NC is likely appropriate but worth confirming. Avoid CC BY-ND if the image may need any modification.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "The Figure dialog isn't opening when choosing Insert → Figure"</strong><br>Solution: Ensure the translator has editing permission for this book. Check project permissions or contact the project administrator.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "An image was inserted but isn't showing in the project"</strong><br>Solution: Images only display in Preview and Formatted view. Change the view using the View menu.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "I typed 'Print' in the Location field, but the typesetter says the image is missing"</strong><br>Solution: The field only recognises single-letter codes. Change "Print" to <strong>P</strong>. To include multiple locations, string the letters together without spaces (e.g., <strong>PE</strong>).</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "What happens if I leave the Location field blank?"</strong><br>Solution: That is perfectly fine — Paratext will assume the image is approved for all formats. You only need to type letters if you want to <em>restrict</em> the image to a specific format.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "The project file size increased after adding images"</strong><br>Solution: This is expected when adding JPG files. If the increase seems excessive, verify JPG files are being used, not high-resolution or uncompressed formats. Open Bible Images may offer larger file sizes by default — resize before inserting if needed.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "Can't decide between black-and-white and colour images"</strong><br>Solution: Black-and-white images are printed inline with text; colour images are grouped together in a signature. Consider budget and reader preferences, and consult with the Translation Consultant and typesetter.</p>
<p><strong>Problem: "Not sure what caption to write"</strong><br>Solution: Keep captions brief but clear. Describe what's happening in the image using vocabulary from the translation text when possible (e.g., "Jesus teaching in the synagogue", "The temple in Jerusalem"). This reinforces the words readers encounter in the Scripture text.</p>
<p><strong>When to ask for help — contact your mentor or supervisor if:</strong> images won't insert after multiple attempts, image files are corrupted or won't display, or you have questions about file sizing or format compatibility.</p>
<p><strong>Contact your Translation Consultant if:</strong> you need guidance on culturally appropriate image selection, the team disagrees on illustration choices, or you are unsure whether a specific CC licence is appropriate for your publication context.</p>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Knowing which problems to escalate and to whom is an important skill. Technical tool problems go to your mentor or supervisor; content, cultural, and licensing decisions go to the Translation Consultant. When in doubt, start with your mentor or supervisor.</p>
</div>
</div>
<hr style="border: 2px solid black;"><!-- Card: Summary and Next Steps -->
<div class="ui-card ui-component" data-widget-type="card" data-type="info" data-widget-id="widget-54-1786465062039">
<div class="drag-handle" title="Drag to reorder" role="button" tabindex="0"><i class="fa-solid fa-grip-dots-vertical"></i></div>
<div class="component-topbar">
<div class="component-type-badge" data-widget-action="menu-toggle"><span class="widget-move-buttons"> <button type="button" class="widget-move-btn move-up" data-widget-action="move-up" title="Move up"> <i class="fas fa-chevron-up"></i> </button> <button type="button" class="widget-move-btn move-down" data-widget-action="move-down" title="Move down"> <i class="fas fa-chevron-down"></i> </button> </span> Card <button class="component-menu-trigger" title="Widget Options"> <i class="fas fa-ellipsis-v"></i> </button></div>
</div>
<div class="card-header">
<h3>Summary and Next Steps</h3>
</div>
<div class="card-content" contenteditable="true">
<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Two image sources are available: the SIL Image Library (offline after download) and Open Bible Images (online, CC-licensed)</li>
<li>The Paratext image library contains over 2,800 indexed illustrations searchable by keyword, Bible reference, or visual style</li>
<li>Always record the correct copyright or attribution text before inserting any image into Paratext</li>
<li>Only insert small jpg files — high-resolution versions are used during typesetting</li>
<li>The 32-page limit is a physical printing constraint set by the funders — plan carefully</li>
<li>Colour and black-and-white images are handled differently in print — discuss with the typesetter early</li>
<li>Image selection is a cultural decision — let the translator and team lead</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>What Comes Next:</strong> Module 2 covers Preparing Back Matter — Maps, Introductions, and Appendices. You'll learn to identify place names for map creation, draft introductions to the New Testament or Bible, and prepare appendices and supplementary materials.</p>
<p><strong>Timeline Guidance:</strong> planning which images to use (1–2 hours), downloading from Open Bible Images if using Option B (30–60 minutes), inserting images (30–60 minutes for 10–15 images), team review and discussion (1–2 hours). Plan for a few days to gather team input on image selection before finalising.</p>
</div>
</div>
<h2>Support Resources</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>WhatsApp/Email:</strong> post questions in your project team WhatsApp group; include screenshots when describing problems</li>
<li><strong>Project Sharing:</strong> use Send/Receive to share the project; include your mentor in project permissions to ask them to verify image placement and quality</li>
<li><strong>Paratext manual:</strong> <a href="https://manual.paratext.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">https://manual.paratext.org/</a> (image library documentation: Section 24.1–24.2)</li>
<li><strong>Open Bible Images:</strong> <a href="https://openbibleimages.org" target="_blank" rel="noopener">https://openbibleimages.org</a></li>
<li>When using Open Bible Images, keep a record of image attributions in a shared spreadsheet for use during typesetting</li>
</ul>
<div style="display: flex; border: 1px solid #2ca02c; border-left: 6px solid #2ca02c; border-radius: 6px; padding: 10px 15px; margin: 1em 0; background-color: #f6fff6; align-items: flex-start; color: #1a3d1a;">
<div style="flex: 1;"><strong>FOR NEW LTCs</strong>
<p>Your role throughout this module is to guide the translator verbally through each step — never touch their keyboard, even when it would be faster. This is their project and they need to develop confidence with the tools. If they're struggling, slow down and explain more carefully rather than doing it for them. This principle applies throughout the entire course.</p>
</div>

Module 2 source code
<h1>Preparing Back Matter: Maps, Introductions, and Appendices</h1>
<p><strong>Duration:</strong>  60 minutes <br><strong>Level:</strong>  Experienced Translator <br><strong>Goal:</strong>  Prepare essential supplementary materials, including map names, introductions, and appendices for publication</p>
<hr><hr><hr style="border: 2px solid black;">
<p><img src="/files/7780768/connect.png" alt="connect" width="294" height="397" style="width: 144px; height: 194px;"></p>
<h2>Introduction</h2>
<p><strong>Time:</strong>  5 minutes</p>
<p>Your translation of the biblical text is complete. But a published Scripture isn't just the translated text—it needs supporting materials that help readers engage with the content effectively.</p>
<p>Maps help readers visualize the geography of biblical events. An introduction orients readers to what they're about to read and why it matters. Appendices provide additional resources like glossaries, chronologies, or explanatory notes.</p>
<p>These "back matter" elements are often overlooked until the typesetter asks for them. This module will guide you through preparing these essential supplementary materials.</p>
<h3>What This Module Covers</h3>
<ul>
<li>Preparing map names using the Map Labeler Plugin</li>
<li>Drafting an introduction to the New Testament or Bible</li>
<li>Creating appendices and supplementary materials</li>
<li>Working with typesetters to determine format requirements</li>
</ul>
<h3>Prerequisites</h3>
<p>Before starting this module, ensure:</p>
<ul>
<li>Your translation text is finalized</li>
<li>You've established contact with your typesetter</li>
<li>You have editing permissions for special books (INT, XXA, etc.) in your Paratext project</li>
<li>You understand basic USFM markers (or have reference materials available)</li>
</ul>
<hr>
<h2>Procedure Walkthrough</h2>
<p><strong>Time:</strong>  40 minutes</p>
<h3>Understanding Back Matter Components</h3>
<p>Back matter includes several types of supplementary content:</p>
<p><strong>Maps:</strong>  Visual representations of biblical geography with place names in your language  <strong>Introduction:</strong>  Explains what the New Testament/Bible is, why it matters, and how to read it  <strong>Appendices:</strong>  Additional helpful materials (glossaries, weights and measures, chronologies, etc.)</p>
<p>Each serves a different purpose, but all help readers engage more fully with the Scripture text.</p>
<h3><strong>Part 1: Preparing Map Names</strong></h3>
<blockquote>
<p>Maps in published Scripture help readers understand the geographical context of biblical events. Place names — Jerusalem, Galilee, Jericho, Rome — need to appear on those maps in your language, spelled consistently with how they appear in the translated text. The Paratext Diagram Labeler plugin is designed to make this possible.</p>
<p><strong>How it works</strong></p>
<p>The plugin reads your project's Biblical Terms renderings and uses them to propose labels for each place on a map. Your job is to review those proposals, make corrections where needed, and ensure that what appears on the map matches what appears in the text. The plugin stores all your labels in a Label Dictionary, so once you've confirmed a place name it stays consistent across all maps in your project.</p>
<p>When you save your work, the labels are exported automatically into files that your typesetter can use directly in their mapping software.</p>
<p><strong>Before you begin</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Paratext 9 should already be installed</li>
<li>It is strongly recommended that you use the Biblical Terms tool to guess renderings for all Names before opening the Labeler — this gives the plugin the best starting point for proposals</li>
<li>Check with your typesetter which file format they need (.mapx for Map Creator, or .idml for Adobe InDesign)</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Getting started</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>Install the Paratext Diagram Labeler plugin if not already installed — download from <a href="https://software.sil.org/labeler">software.sil.org/labeler</a></li>
<li>Work through your maps using the plugin, reviewing and confirming place name labels</li>
<li>Save your work — labels are exported automatically to your project's shared folder and included in Send/Receive</li>
<li>Notify your typesetter that labels are ready</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>For detailed guidance</strong> , refer to the full user guide at <a href="https://software.sil.org/labeler/learn">software.sil.org/labeler/learn</a> , which covers installation, the interface, working with labels, and exporting finished maps.</p>
<p>⚠️ <em> Note: This plugin is currently in beta. If you encounter problems or need help installing it, contact your Language Technology Consultant. </em></p>
<p> </p>
</blockquote>
<p>Part 2: Drafting an Introduction to the NT/Bible</p>
<p>An introduction helps readers understand what they're about to read. It's especially important for readers encountering Scripture for the first time.</p>
<p><strong>Purpose of the Introduction:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Explains what the New Testament/Bible is</li>
<li>Provides historical and cultural context</li>
<li>Helps readers understand how to approach the text</li>
<li>May explain translation decisions or text organization</li>
</ul>
<h4>Step 1: Access the Introduction Book</h4>
<ol>
<li>
<p>In Paratext, navigate to the book  <strong>INT</strong>  (Introduction)</p>
<ul>
<li>This is a special book code specifically for introductory material</li>
<li>If you don't see INT in your book list, you may need to enable it in project settings</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Ensure the book has a  <strong>\h</strong>  (header) line</p>
<ul>
<li>This line identifies the book</li>
<li>Example:  <code>\h Introduction to the New Testament</code></li>
</ul>
</li>
</ol>
<h4>Step 2: Structure Your Introduction</h4>
<p>Use these USFM markers to format your introduction properly:</p>
<blockquote><strong>Tip:</strong>   <br>
<p>If you're new to USFM markers, think of them like formatting codes. \mt1 = Main Title (level 1), \is = Introduction Section heading, \ip = Introduction Paragraph. Each marker tells the typesetter how to format that content.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>\mt1</strong>  - Main Title</p>
<ul>
<li>Use for the introduction title</li>
<li>Example:  <code>\mt1 Introduction to the New Testament</code></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>\is</strong>  - Introduction Section Heading</p>
<ul>
<li>Use for section headings within your introduction</li>
<li>Example:  <code>\is What is the New Testament?</code></li>
<li>Example:  <code>\is How to Read This Book</code></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>\ip</strong>  - Introduction Paragraph</p>
<ul>
<li>Use for body text paragraphs</li>
<li>Most of your content will use this marker</li>
</ul>
<h4>Step 3: Content to Include</h4>
<p>Consider including sections on:</p>
<p><strong>What is the New Testament/Bible?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Basic explanation of what Scripture is</li>
<li>How it was written and preserved</li>
<li>Its significance for faith and life</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>The Structure of the New Testament</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Brief overview of the four Gospels</li>
<li>Acts and the early church</li>
<li>Paul's letters and general epistles</li>
<li>Revelation</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>How to Read the New Testament</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Guidance for first-time readers</li>
<li>Understanding different types of literature (narrative, letters, prophecy)</li>
<li>Encouragement and practical tips</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>About This Translation</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Brief note about your translation work</li>
<li>Any important translation decisions readers should know</li>
<li>How to use cross-references, footnotes, and other helps</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Tips for Writing a Good Introduction:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Write in clear, accessible language</li>
<li>Keep it relatively brief (2-4 pages typically)</li>
<li>Focus on helping readers, not impressing scholars</li>
<li>Have it reviewed by community advisors and your Translation Consultant</li>
<li>Consider your readers' familiarity with Scripture</li>
</ul>
<blockquote>✅  <strong>Best Practice:</strong> <br>
<p>Read introductions from published Bibles in related languages for inspiration, but write in your own voice for your specific audience. What works for one culture may not work for another.</p>
</blockquote>
<h4>Step 4: Example Structure</h4>
<p>Here's a sample structure using proper markers:</p>
<pre><code>\h Introduction to the New Testament
\mt1 Introduction to the New Testament

\is What is the New Testament?
\ip The New Testament is the second part of the Christian Bible. It tells the story of Jesus Christ and the early church...

\is The Four Gospels
\ip The first four books—Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John—tell the story of Jesus' life, teaching, death, and resurrection...

\is How to Read This Book
\ip You can start reading anywhere, but many people begin with the Gospel of Mark...
</code></pre>
<h4>Step 5: Review and Refinement</h4>
<ol>
<li>Draft your introduction content</li>
<li>Review it with your translation team</li>
<li>Share with community advisors for feedback</li>
<li>Submit to your Translation Consultant for approval</li>
<li>Make revisions based on feedback</li>
<li>Finalize the text in the INT book</li>
</ol>
<hr>
<h3>Part 3: Creating Appendices</h3>
<p>Appendices provide additional helpful resources for readers. Common appendices include:</p>
<p><strong>Glossary:</strong>  Definitions of difficult or important terms  <strong>Weights and Measures:</strong>  Explanations of biblical measurements  <strong>Chronology:</strong>  Timeline of biblical events  <strong>Maps:</strong>  (In addition to maps in the text)  <strong>Names Index:</strong>  Alphabetical list of important names  <strong>Topical Index:</strong>  Key topics and where to find them</p>
<h4>Choosing Appendix Content</h4>
<p><strong>Not every publication needs every type of appendix.</strong>  Consider:</p>
<ul>
<li>What will help your readers most?</li>
<li>What's your page budget?</li>
<li>What's culturally appropriate and useful?</li>
<li>What does your typesetter recommend?</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Consult with your Translation Consultant and typesetter</strong>  about which appendices to include.</p>
<h4>Where to Create Appendices in Paratext</h4>
<p>Paratext provides several book codes for appendices:</p>
<p><strong>General Appendix Books:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>XXA</strong>  - Appendix A</li>
<li><strong>XXB</strong>  - Appendix B</li>
<li><strong>XXC</strong>  - Appendix C</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Specific Purpose Books:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>BAK</strong>  - Back Matter</li>
<li><strong>OTH</strong>  - Other Matter</li>
<li><strong>TDX</strong>  - Topical Index</li>
<li><strong>NDX</strong>  - Names Index</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>⚠️ Important: </strong></p>
<p>Check with your typesetter about which book codes to use. Different publishing systems may have different preferences.</p>
<p>Always coordinate with your typesetter <strong>BEFORE</strong> creating extensive appendix content. Nothing is more frustrating than spending hours formatting an appendix only to learn the typesetter needs it in a completely different format or book code.</p>
<h4>Creating Appendix Content</h4>
<ol>
<li>Navigate to the appropriate appendix book (e.g., XXA)</li>
<li>
<p>Use standard USFM markers:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>\h</strong>  - Header line (book identification)</li>
<li><strong>\mt1</strong>  - Main title</li>
<li><strong>\s</strong>  - Section headings</li>
<li><strong>\p</strong>  - Paragraphs</li>
<li><strong>\li</strong>  - List items (for indexes)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Create your content following your typesetter's specifications</p>
</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Example Glossary Structure:</strong></p>
<pre><code>\h Glossary
\mt1 Glossary of Important Terms

\s A
\p Atonement - The act of making amends for sin...

\s B
\p Baptism - A ceremony using water to show...
</code></pre>
<h4>Working with Your Typesetter</h4>
<p><strong>Before creating extensive appendix content:</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>Contact your typesetter</li>
<li>Ask which appendices they recommend</li>
<li>Get format specifications</li>
<li>Confirm which Paratext book codes to use</li>
<li>Ask about page limits or other constraints</li>
</ol>
<p>This prevents wasted work if format requirements change.</p>
<hr>
<h2>Verification Checklist</h2>
<p><strong>Time:</strong>  5 minutes</p>
<p>After completing this module, verify that you can:</p>
<p><strong>Map Names:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>[ ] Understand the purpose of providing map names</li>
<li>[ ] [Access and use the Map Labeler Plugin - details to be added]</li>
<li>[ ] Export map names for the typesetter</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Introduction:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>[ ] Navigate to the INT book in Paratext</li>
<li>[ ] Use correct USFM markers (\h, \mt1, \is, \ip)</li>
<li>[ ] Structure an introduction with multiple sections</li>
<li>[ ] Include helpful content appropriate for your readers</li>
<li>[ ] Submit introduction for consultant review</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Appendices:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>[ ] Know which appendix book codes are available (XXA, XXB, XXC, BAK, OTH, TDX, NDX)</li>
<li>[ ] Understand the need to coordinate with your typesetter</li>
<li>[ ] Create basic appendix content using proper markers</li>
<li>[ ] Format content appropriately for different appendix types</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Quality Checks:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Introduction is clear and helpful for target readers</li>
<li>Content uses culturally appropriate examples and explanations</li>
<li>USFM markers are used correctly</li>
<li>Typesetter has confirmed format requirements</li>
<li>Translation Consultant has reviewed and approved content</li>
</ul>
<hr>
<h2>Troubleshooting</h2>
<p><strong>Time:</strong>  5 minutes</p>
<h3>Common Issues and Solutions</h3>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "I don't see the INT book in my project"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  The INT book may need to be added to your project. Go to Project &gt; Book Names and Abbreviations, and ensure INT is enabled. Contact your Language Technology Consultant if you need help.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "I'm not sure what USFM markers to use for my introduction"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  Use \mt1 for titles, \is for section headings, and \ip for paragraphs. For more complex formatting, consult the USFM documentation or ask your Language Technology Consultant.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "My introduction seems too long/too short"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  Typical introductions are 2-4 pages. Focus on what readers need to know. Get feedback from your Translation Consultant on appropriate length.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "I don't know which appendices to include"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  Start by asking your typesetter what they recommend. Common choices are glossary and maps. Your Translation Consultant can also provide guidance based on similar projects.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "The typesetter is asking for content in a different format than what I created"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  This is why it's important to coordinate with the typesetter BEFORE creating extensive content. Ask for their specifications and reformat as needed. Your Language Technology Consultant can help with technical formatting issues.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Problem:</strong>  "My appendix content isn't displaying properly in Paratext"</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Solution:</strong>  Check that you're using the correct USFM markers. Make sure your book has a \h line. Switch to Preview or Formatted view to see how it will appear. Contact your Language Technology Consultant for formatting help.</li>
</ul>
<h3>When to Ask for Help</h3>
<p><strong>Contact your Language Technology Consultant if:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>You can't access special books like INT or XXA in your project (technical setup)</li>
<li>USFM markers aren't working as expected (technical formatting)</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Contact your Translation Consultant if:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>You need guidance on introduction content (translation and cultural appropriateness)</li>
<li>You're unsure which appendices are needed for your audience (publishing decisions)</li>
<li>You need feedback on drafts before finalizing (content review)</li>
</ul>
<hr>
<h2>Summary and Next Steps</h2>
<p><strong>Time:</strong>  3 minutes</p>
<h3>Key Takeaways</h3>
<ul>
<li>Maps require identifying place names in your language using the Map Labeler Plugin</li>
<li>Introductions are created in the special INT book using \mt1, \is, and \ip markers</li>
<li>Appendices can be created in XXA, XXB, XXC or specialized books like BAK, TDX, NDX</li>
<li>Always coordinate with your typesetter before creating extensive back matter content</li>
<li>All introductory and appendix materials should be reviewed by your Translation Consultant</li>
</ul>
<h3>What Comes Next</h3>
<p>After preparing back matter, the next module covers  <strong>Comprehensive Checking Workflows: Parallel Passages and Verification</strong> . You'll learn to:</p>
<ul>
<li>Check parallel passages for consistency</li>
<li>Verify that all required checks have been completed</li>
<li>Use Assignments and Progress to track completion</li>
<li>Run comprehensive check reports</li>
</ul>
<h3>Timeline Guidance</h3>
<p>Preparing back matter typically takes:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Map names:</strong>  2-4 hours (depending on number of places)</li>
<li><strong>Introduction draft:</strong>  4-8 hours</li>
<li><strong>Introduction review and revision:</strong>  1-2 weeks (waiting for feedback)</li>
<li><strong>Appendix creation:</strong>  Varies widely by type and length</li>
<li><strong>Coordination with typesetter:</strong>  Ongoing throughout the process</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Plan ahead:</strong>  </p>
<p>Back matter preparation often reveals the need for additional translation decisions. Build in time for team discussion and consultant feedback.</p>
<hr>

<li>For technical issues, contact Language Technology Consultant</li>
<li>For content/translation issues, contact Translation Consultant</li>
</ul>

<ul>

</ul>

</ul>

Module 3: source code
<hr />
<h1>Comprehensive Checking Workflows: Parallel Passages and Verification</h1>
<p>
  <strong>Duration:</strong>
  &nbsp;60 minutes
  <br />
  <strong>Level:</strong>
  &nbsp;Experienced Translator
  <br />
  <strong>Goal:</strong>
  &nbsp;Verify parallel passages are consistent and confirm all required checks have been completed before publication
</p>
<hr />
<hr />
<p><img src="/files/7780768/connect.png" alt="connect" width="294" height="397" /></p>
<h2>Introduction</h2>
<p>
  <strong>Time:</strong>
  &nbsp;5 minutes
</p>
<p>
  You think you're finished. The translation is done, consultants have approved it, illustrations are added, back matter
  is prepared. But have you checked&nbsp;
  <em>everything</em>
  ?
</p>
<p>
  One of the most embarrassing discoveries after publication is finding inconsistencies between parallel passages—the
  same story told differently in different Gospels, but translated with different vocabulary in your language. Or
  discovering you missed an entire category of checks and there are still formatting errors throughout.
</p>
<p>
  This module guides you through the systematic verification process to ensure your translation is truly ready for
  publication. You'll check parallel passages for consistency and run comprehensive verification to confirm nothing has
  been missed.
</p>
<h3>What This Module Covers</h3>
<ul>
  <li>Checking parallel passages for consistency</li>
  <li>Using Assignments and Progress to verify completion</li>
  <li>Running comprehensive basic checks across all books</li>
  <li>Performing word list checks (spelling, similar words, joined/split words)</li>
  <li>Understanding when checks are truly complete</li>
</ul>
<h3>Prerequisites</h3>
<p>Before starting this module, ensure:</p>
<ul>
  <li>You're familiar with Paratext's checking tools</li>
  <li>You have editing permissions for all books being published</li>
  <li>Your project has access to the Parallel Passages module (if available)</li>
  <li>You understand your project's checking requirements</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p><img src="/files/7780768/content.png" alt="content" width="341" height="397" /></p>
<h2>Procedure Walkthrough</h2>
<p>
  <strong>Time:</strong>
  &nbsp;40 minutes
</p>
<h3>Part 1: Check Parallel Passages</h3>
<p>
  Parallel passages are portions of Scripture that appear in multiple books, often with similar or identical wording.
  The Gospels contain many parallel passages—the same events and teachings recorded by different Gospel writers.
</p>
<p>
  <strong>Why this matters:</strong>
  &nbsp;Readers notice when the same event is described using different vocabulary in your language. While some
  variation is acceptable (different Gospel writers emphasized different details), major inconsistencies can confuse
  readers or make them question the translation's accuracy.
</p>
<blockquote>
  ⚠️&nbsp;
  <strong>Important:</strong>
  &nbsp;
  <br />
  <blockquote>
    <p>
      The goal is NOT to make all parallel passages word-for-word identical. Gospel writers sometimes emphasized
      different aspects. The goal is consistency in key vocabulary while respecting the original writer's intentions.
    </p>
  </blockquote>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Examples of parallel passages:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>The Lord's Prayer (Matthew 6:9-13 and Luke 11:2-4)</li>
  <li>The feeding of the 5,000 (Matthew 14:13-21, Mark 6:30-44, Luke 9:10-17, John 6:1-15)</li>
  <li>The Beatitudes (Matthew 5:3-12 and Luke 6:20-23)</li>
  <li>Jesus calming the storm (Matthew 8:23-27, Mark 4:35-41, Luke 8:22-25)</li>
</ul>
<h4>Using the Parallel Passages Module</h4>

<strong>Note:</strong>
&nbsp;
<br />
<p>
  For detailed instructions on using the Parallel Passages tool, see Module 23 "Compare Parallel Passages" in the full
  Paratext training course.
</p>

<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><strong>Basic process:</strong></p>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      Open the&nbsp;
      <strong>Parallel Passages</strong>
      &nbsp;tool in Paratext
    </p>
    <ul>
      <li>
        <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
        &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
        <strong>Tools</strong>
        &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
        <strong>Parallel Passages</strong>
      </li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>The tool will display groups of parallel passages</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Each group shows the same event/teaching across different books</li>
      <li>You can see your translation in all parallel locations side-by-side</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Review each set of parallels for consistency</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Are key terms translated the same way?</li>
      <li>Are there appropriate reasons for any differences?</li>
      <li>Would readers be confused by the variations?</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Make adjustments as needed</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Harmonize key vocabulary where appropriate</li>
      <li>Maintain intentional differences where Gospel writers emphasized different aspects</li>
      <li>Document your decisions for future reference</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Mark passages as reviewed</p>
    <ul>
      <li>The tool tracks which parallel sets you've examined</li>
      <li>This helps you work systematically through all parallels</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
</ol>

<strong>Key principle:</strong>
<br />
<p>
  The goal is consistency in key vocabulary, not word-for-word identical translation. Gospel writers sometimes
  emphasized different aspects of the same event, and your translation can reflect those differences while maintaining
  clarity for readers.
</p>

<p><strong>If the Parallel Passages tool is not available:</strong></p>
<p>You can manually check common parallel passages by:</p>
<ol>
  <li>Opening multiple windows in Paratext showing the parallel texts</li>
  <li>Reading through them side by side</li>
  <li>Noting vocabulary differences</li>
  <li>Making appropriate adjustments</li>
</ol>
<hr />
<h3>Part 2: Verify All Checks Are Complete</h3>
<p>Now you'll systematically verify that every required check has been completed for your publication.</p>
<h4>Method 1: Current Book Only</h4>
<p>If you're verifying a single book:</p>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      Open&nbsp;
      <strong>Assignments and Progress</strong>
    </p>
    <ul>
      <li>
        <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
        &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
        <strong>Project</strong>
        &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
        <strong>Assignments and Progress</strong>
      </li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Select the book you're checking</p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Review the progress indicators</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Look at each stage and check type</li>
      <li>Confirm there are green checkmarks (completed) or acceptable status</li>
      <li>Investigate any red X marks or warning indicators</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Click on any incomplete items</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Review the specific issues</li>
      <li>Determine if they need to be resolved before publication</li>
      <li>Consult with your Translation Consultant if unclear</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
</ol>
<p><strong>The Assignments and Progress tool shows:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Which checks have been run</li>
  <li>Whether issues were found</li>
  <li>Current status of each checking category</li>
  <li>Who is responsible for each stage</li>
</ul>
<h4>Method 2: Multiple Books</h4>
<p>
  If you're publishing multiple books (typical for New Testament publication), you need a more comprehensive approach:
</p>
<p><strong>Step 1: Run Comprehensive Basic Checks</strong></p>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Tools</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Run Basic Checks</strong>
    </p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>In the dialog that appears:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>
        <strong>Make sure ALL checks are ticked</strong>
        &nbsp;(selected)
        <ul>
          <li>Chapter/verse numbers</li>
          <li>Markers</li>
          <li>Characters</li>
          <li>Punctuation</li>
          <li>Matched pairs</li>
          <li>Repeated words</li>
          <li>Capitalization</li>
          <li>References</li>
          <li>Quotations</li>
          <li>Numbers</li>
          <li>[Any other checks available in your Paratext version]</li>
        </ul>
      </li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p><strong>Make sure all books to be published are chosen</strong></p>
    <ul>
      <li>Click "Select Books" if needed</li>
      <li>Verify every book that will be published is selected</li>
      <li>Don't skip any books</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>
      Click&nbsp;
      <strong>OK</strong>
    </p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p><strong>Review the results carefully</strong></p>
    <ul>
      <li>The results window shows all issues found</li>
      <li>Each issue is listed with its location and description</li>
      <li>Some issues may be intentional (and can be ignored)</li>
      <li>Others need correction</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p><strong>Correct any errors</strong></p>
    <p>1.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Work through the list systematically</p>
    <p>2.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Fix genuine errors</p>
    <p>3.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Document your decisions</p>
    <p><b>If you find many errors in a specific check (e.g., punctuation, repeated words):</b></p>
    <p>You may want to update the relevant inventory to help resolve issues more efficiently:</p>
    <p>
      1.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;
      <b>≡ Tab</b>
      &gt;
      <b>Tools</b>
      &gt;
      <b>Inventories</b>
    </p>
    <p>
      2.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Select the specific inventory related to your errors
      (e.g., Punctuation Inventory, Repeated Words Inventory)
    </p>
    <p>
      1.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; There are 9 separate inventories; you don't need to
      update all of them
    </p>
    <p>3.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Open the inventory and sort by the Status column</p>
    <p>4.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Look for items marked as "Unknown"</p>
    <p>
      5.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Review each unknown item and mark it as either Valid or
      Invalid
    </p>
    <p>1.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Valid: This is acceptable in your translation</p>
    <p>2.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Invalid: This is an error that needs correction</p>
    <p>
      6.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; After updating the inventory, re-run the relevant check
      to see reduced errors
    </p>

    <b>Tip:</b>
    <br />
    Updating inventories is a manual review process that requires your attention—review each item thoughtfully. At this
    final stage of a project, updating inventories is usually quite fast since most items have already been marked as
    valid or invalid during earlier stages.
  </li>
</ol>
<blockquote>
  ❌&nbsp;
  <strong>Common Mistake:</strong>
  &nbsp;
  <br />
  <p>
    Don't dismiss all errors as "acceptable variations" without careful review. Each issue should be examined and a
    conscious decision made. Laziness at this stage shows up in your published Bible.
  </p>
</blockquote>
<h3>Part 3: Word List Checks</h3>
<p>
  In addition to basic checks, you need to run specialised word list checks to catch spelling and word formation issues.
</p>
<h4>Check 1: Spell Check - All Checks</h4>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Tools</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Spell Check</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>All Checks</strong>
    </p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>The spell checker will identify:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Words not in your spelling dictionary</li>
      <li>Possible misspellings</li>
      <li>Inconsistent spelling of the same word</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>For each flagged word, decide:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>
        <strong>Correct spelling:</strong>
        &nbsp;Fix the misspelling
      </li>
      <li>
        <strong>Add to dictionary:</strong>
        &nbsp;If it's spelled correctly but not in dictionary
      </li>
      <li>
        <strong>Ignore:</strong>
        &nbsp;If it's a proper name or specialized term you don't want in dictionary
      </li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Work through all flagged items systematically</p>
  </li>
</ol>
<blockquote>
  ✅&nbsp;
  <strong>Best Practice:</strong>
  &nbsp;
  <br />
  <p>
    If you see the same misspelling multiple times, use "Replace All" to fix it everywhere at once.
    <strong>But be careful</strong>
    —verify it's actually an error before batch-replacing.
    <strong>One wrong "Replace All" can create dozens of new errors!</strong>
  </p>
</blockquote>
<h4>Check 2: Find Similar Words</h4>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Tools</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Find Similar Words</strong>
    </p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>This tool identifies words that are very similar but not identical</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Helps catch inconsistent spelling of the same word</li>
      <li>Example: "Jerusalem" spelled three different ways</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Review each pair of similar words:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Are they the same word spelled inconsistently?</li>
      <li>Or are they legitimately different words?</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Make necessary corrections for consistency</p>
  </li>
</ol>

<strong>Note</strong>
<p>
  <strong>This is crucial for proper names</strong>
  &nbsp;where slight spelling variations are especially noticeable to readers.
</p>

<h4>Check 3: Find Incorrectly Joined or Split Words</h4>
<ol>
  <li>
    <p>
      <strong>≡ Tab</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Tools</strong>
      &nbsp;&gt;&nbsp;
      <strong>Find Incorrectly Joined or Split Words</strong>
    </p>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>This check identifies:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Words that might be incorrectly joined together (nospaceerror)</li>
      <li>Words that might be incorrectly split (two parts separated)</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>
    <p>Review each flagged instance:</p>
    <ul>
      <li>Should it be one word or two?</li>
      <li>Is the current form correct for your language?</li>
      <li>Make corrections as needed</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Common issues this catches:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Compound words sometimes written as one word, sometimes two</li>
  <li>Typographical errors where spaces were omitted or added</li>
  <li>Inconsistencies in how your language handles certain constructions</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2>Verification Checklist</h2>
<p>
  <strong>Time:</strong>
  &nbsp;5 minutes
</p>
<p>After completing this module, verify that you have:</p>
<p><strong>Parallel Passages:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>[ ] Reviewed all major parallel passage sets</li>
  <li>[ ] Checked consistency of key vocabulary across parallels</li>
  <li>[ ] Made appropriate harmonization where needed</li>
  <li>[ ] Documented decisions about intentional differences</li>
  <li>[ ] Marked parallel passage review as complete</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Comprehensive Checks:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>[ ] Updated all inventories before running checks</li>
  <li>[ ] Run basic checks with ALL check types selected</li>
  <li>[ ] Run basic checks on ALL books to be published</li>
  <li>[ ] Reviewed every issue in the results list</li>
  <li>[ ] Corrected genuine errors</li>
  <li>[ ] Documented decisions about acceptable variations</li>
  <li>[ ] Verified Assignments and Progress shows completion</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Word List Checks:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>[ ] Run Spell Check &gt; All Checks</li>
  <li>[ ] Reviewed and resolved all spelling issues</li>
  <li>[ ] Run Find Similar Words</li>
  <li>[ ] Corrected inconsistent spellings</li>
  <li>[ ] Run Find Incorrectly Joined or Split Words</li>
  <li>[ ] Resolved word spacing issues</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Overall Status:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>[ ] Zero unresolved errors in basic checks (or all exceptions documented)</li>
  <li>[ ] No spelling issues remaining</li>
  <li>[ ] Word consistency verified</li>
  <li>[ ] Translation Consultant has confirmed readiness for publication</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>To verify completion:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Send/Receive your project</li>
  <li>Ask a colleague to run the same checks and compare results</li>
  <li>Share with your Translation Consultant for final verification</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h1>Troubleshooting</h1>
<p>
  <strong>Time:</strong>
  &nbsp;5 minutes
</p>
<h3>Common Issues and Solutions</h3>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"Basic checks are showing hundreds of errors"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    This is unusual at Stage 6—most errors should have been caught in earlier stages. Update the relevant inventories to
    mark valid items, then re-run the checks. If you still have many genuine errors after updating inventories, you may
    need to return to earlier checking stages before proceeding to publication.
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"I fixed an error but it's still showing in the check results"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    &nbsp;After making corrections, re-run the specific check. Basic checks don't automatically update. Also verify you
    saved your changes before re-running.
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"Some 'errors' are actually correct in my language"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    Most checks have a way to define what's correct for your language:
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; For checks like Punctuation, Characters,
      Capitalization, Repeated Words: Open the relevant inventory and mark the item as Valid
    </p>
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; For Quotations: Check your Quotation rules
      settings (Project Settings &gt; Quotation rules)
    </p>
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; For Numbers: Check your Number settings
      (Project Settings &gt; Number settings)
    </p>
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; For References: Check your Scripture reference
      settings (Project Settings &gt; Scripture reference settings)
    </p>
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; For spell check: Add the word to your wordlist
    </p>
    <p>
      –&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; If you're unsure whether something is truly
      correct or if settings are configured properly, document the issue and discuss with your team or Translation
      Consultant.
    </p>
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"Spell check flags every proper name"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    &nbsp;You'll need to add proper names as renderings to the names in your Biblical Terms list. Consult another
    Language Technology Consultant for the best approach, or contact Paratext using the Give Feedback command on the
    Paratext menu under Help.
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"Inventories take forever to update"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    &nbsp;This is normal for large projects. Run inventory updates during a break or at the end of the day. The larger
    your project, the longer it takes.
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"I'm not sure if an issue needs fixing or is acceptable"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    &nbsp;When in doubt, ask your Translation Consultant. It's better to check than to publish with questionable issues.
    Document the question and their response for future reference.
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Problem:</strong>
  &nbsp;"Different team members are getting different check results"
</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Solution:</strong>
    &nbsp;Ensure everyone has the latest project version (Send/Receive). Also, verify everyone has updated inventories.
    If results still differ, there may be a project configuration issue—contact your mentor—or, if needed, report the
    problem: on the Paratext menu, go to
    <strong>Help</strong>
    , then choose
    <strong>Give Feedback</strong>
    .
  </li>
</ul>
<h3>When to Ask for Help</h3>
<p><strong>Contact a Language Technology Consultant if:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Checking tools aren't working properly (technical issues)</li>
  <li>Inventory updates fail or produce errors (system problems)</li>
  <li>You have questions about what specific checks are looking for (tool functionality)</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Contact your Translation Consultant if:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>You're unsure whether an issue is a real error or acceptable variation (translation decision)</li>
  <li>Parallel passages show major inconsistencies and you need guidance (content question)</li>
  <li>You need advice on how to resolve conflicting translation choices (linguistic issue)</li>
  <li>You want final verification that checks are complete (publication approval)</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2>Summary and Next Steps</h2>
<p>
  <strong>Time:</strong>
  &nbsp;3 minutes
</p>
<h3>Key Takeaways</h3>
<ul>
  <li>Parallel passages should use consistent key vocabulary to avoid confusing readers</li>
  <li>Comprehensive checking requires ALL checks run on ALL books being published</li>
  <li>Update inventories before running checks to ensure accuracy</li>
  <li>Word list checks (spelling, similar words, joined/split words) catch issues basic checks miss</li>
  <li>
    Every issue found should be reviewed and a conscious decision made—not all need fixing, but all need evaluation
  </li>
  <li>Assignments and Progress tool tracks completion status</li>
</ul>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<hr />
<p>
  <em><img src="/files/7780768/mceclip0(7).png" width="139" height="194" /></em>
</p>
<p>
  After comprehensive verification, the next module covers&nbsp;
  <strong>Proper Names and Biblical Terms Final Check</strong>
  . You'll learn to:
</p>
<ul>
  <li>Review the Major Biblical Terms list for completeness</li>
  <li>Ensure all proper names have renderings</li>
  <li>Filter for missing renderings</li>
  <li>Make final decisions on biblical terminology</li>
</ul>
<h3>Timeline Guidance</h3>
<p>Comprehensive checking typically takes:</p>
<ul>
  <li>
    <strong>Parallel passages review:</strong>
    &nbsp;4-8 hours (depending on number of books)
  </li>
  <li>
    <strong>Running comprehensive basic checks:</strong>
    &nbsp;1-2 hours (depending on number of issues found)
  </li>
  <li>
    <strong>Resolving check issues:</strong>
    &nbsp;1-3 weeks (depending on number of issues found)
  </li>
  <li>
    <strong>Word list checks:</strong>
    &nbsp;2-4 hours
  </li>
  <li>
    <strong>Final verification:</strong>
    &nbsp;1-2 days
  </li>
</ul>
<p>
  <strong>Important:</strong>
  &nbsp;Don't rush this stage. Issues found after publication are extremely costly and embarrassing to fix. Take the
  time to verify everything properly.
</p>
<p>
  <strong>Realistic expectation:</strong>
  &nbsp;If this is your first comprehensive check of the full publication set, plan for 2-4 weeks to work through all
  issues. Subsequent publications go faster because you've learned what to watch for.
</p>
<hr />
<h2>Support Resources</h2>
<h3>Getting Help</h3>
<p><strong>WhatsApp/Email Support:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Post questions in your project team WhatsApp group</li>
  <li>For checking tool questions, contact a Language Technology Consultant</li>
  <li>For translation decision questions, contact Translation Consultant</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Project Sharing for Verification:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>Use Send/Receive to share your project</li>
  <li>Include your Translation Consultant in project permissions</li>
  <li>Ask them to verify check completion before proceeding to publication</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Related Documentation:</strong></p>
<ul>
  <li>
    Full Paratext manual:&nbsp;
    <a href="https://manual.paratext.org/">https://manual.paratext.org/</a>
  </li>
  <li>Parallel passages module: Section 23</li>
  <li>Basic checks: Section 12</li>
  <li>Word list checks: Section 19</li>
  <li>Comprehensive checking: Section 24.5-24.6</li>
</ul>
<h3>Additional Resources</h3>
<ul>
  <li>Create a checking schedule to work through issues systematically</li>
  <li>
    While different team members may prepare or research different aspects beforehand, final checks are completed as a
    group session with one person's screen projected. This collaborative approach ensures accuracy and allows the team
    to resolve questions in real-time
  </li>
  <li>Document common issues and your team's decisions for consistency</li>
  <li>Keep a log of questions asked to consultants and their responses</li>
  <li>Maintain a list of acceptable exceptions for future reference</li>
</ul>

Module 4 source code
====
<h1 id="proper-names-and-biblical-terms-final-check" style="color: rgb(0, 0, 0); font-family: 'Times New Roman'; font-style: normal; font-variant-ligatures: normal; font-variant-caps: normal; letter-spacing: normal; orphans: 2; text-align: start; text-indent: 0px; text-transform: none; widows: 2; word-spacing: 0px; -webkit-text-stroke-width: 0px; white-space: normal; text-decoration-thickness: initial; text-decoration-style: initial; text-decoration-color: initial;">Proper Names and Biblical Terms Final Check</h1>
<p style="color: rgb(0, 0, 0); font-family: 'Times New Roman'; font-size: medium; font-style: normal; font-variant-ligatures: normal; font-variant-caps: normal; font-weight: 400; letter-spacing: normal; orphans: 2; text-align: start; text-indent: 0px; text-transform: none; widows: 2; word-spacing: 0px; -webkit-text-stroke-width: 0px; white-space: normal; text-decoration-thickness: initial; text-decoration-style: initial; text-decoration-color: initial;"><strong>Duration:</strong><span> </span>60 minutes<br><strong>Level:</strong><span> </span>Experienced Translator<br><strong>Goal:</strong><span> </span>Ensure all proper names and major biblical terms have approved renderings before publication</p>
<hr style="border: 2px solid black;">
<p><img src="/files/7780768/connect.png" alt="connect" width="294" height="397" style="width: 144px; height: 194px;"></p>
<p style="color: rgb(0, 0, 0); font-family: 'Times New Roman'; font-size: medium; font-style: normal; font-variant-ligatures: normal; font-variant-caps: normal; font-weight: 400; letter-spacing: normal; orphans: 2; text-align: start; text-indent: 0px; text-transform: none; widows: 2; word-spacing: 0px; -webkit-text-stroke-width: 0px; white-space: normal; text-decoration-thickness: initial; text-decoration-style: initial; text-decoration-color: initial;"><strong>Time:</strong><span> </span>5 minutes</p>
